Requested as "SMS alerts on every move" (request accepted, delivery started, delivery ended) plus area-wide disaster alerts to victims based on their location. Real SMS was initially ruled out as a scope cut (Twilio needs a paid/verified business account, or so it seemed at the time) and **Firebase Cloud Messaging** (free, browser/app push) was built as the sole channel instead. **SMS was later reopened and built too, alongside FCM, not instead of it** — see "Real SMS (Text.lk)" below for why and how.

- **`server/src/utils/notifications.js`** — `sendNotificationToUser(uid, {title, body, data})`, the one shared helper every trigger point below calls. Looks up `users/{uid}.fcmTokens` (an array — one user can have several devices/browsers registered), sends via `admin.messaging().sendEachForMulticast()`, and prunes any token FCM reports as dead. **Two dead-token error codes matter, not just one** — found by testing directly rather than guessing: `messaging/registration-token-not-registered` / `messaging/invalid-registration-token` (a token that was once valid and has since expired/been uninstalled) and, separately, `messaging/invalid-argument` (FCM's actual code for a malformed/non-token string — confirmed by sending a deliberately garbage token and inspecting the real response). Fails soft everywhere — a missing profile, zero tokens, or a send error never breaks the caller's actual work (verifying a request, updating a delivery).
- **`POST /api/users/fcm-token`** (any authed user) — body `{token}`, appends to `fcmTokens` via `arrayUnion` (re-registering the same token is a no-op). No new Firestore rule needed — `users/{userId}` already allows the owner to write their own doc.
- **Three trigger points, interpreting the literal ask onto this system's actual delivery lifecycle** (documented here in case a future session wonders why these three and not others): "request accepted" → `PATCH /api/requests/:id/verify` and `/bulk-verify` when `approve === true` (this is the victim's first "good news" moment, not the volunteer's own delivery-assignment accept/reject, which is a separate concept); "started delivery" → `PATCH /api/deliveries/:id/status` when the new status is `picked_up`; "end of delivery" → the same route when status is `delivered`, **and** `PATCH /api/deliveries/:id/self-deliver` (self-delivery skips `picked_up` entirely, so it only ever fires the `delivered` copy). Deliberately notifies at `delivered` ("scan to confirm") rather than `confirmed` — the victim is the one who triggers `confirmed` themselves by scanning, so telling them what they just did would be redundant; the useful moment to interrupt them is when the QR is ready to scan, not after.
- **Frontend (`web/src/lib/firebase.ts`, `lib/notifications.ts`, `components/NotificationSetup.tsx`, `public/firebase-messaging-sw.js`):** `firebase.ts` exports `messagingPromise` (an `isSupported()`-gated promise, since `getMessaging()` throws outright in browsers without Push API support — Safari <16.4, non-HTTPS/non-localhost — rather than returning `null` the way the rest of this SDK does). `NotificationSetup.tsx` is rendered once in `DashboardLayout.tsx` (so every logged-in role gets it, not just victims — no reason to restrict it given volunteers/donors could get their own notification types later) and handles the whole lifecycle: silently re-registers the token on every login if permission was already granted in a past session, shows a one-line dismissible opt-in bar if permission hasn't been decided yet (`Notification.permission === "default"`), and renders a toast for foreground messages (background messages are handled by the service worker instead — FCM never calls `onMessage` for a page that's frontmost, only `onBackgroundMessage` in the SW for everything else).
- **`public/firebase-messaging-sw.js` must live at the site root** (FCM's hard convention, not configurable) and, being a plain script the browser loads directly rather than something Vite bundles, **can't read `import.meta.env`** — its Firebase config values are hand-duplicated from `web/.env` with a comment explaining why. This is fine, not a leak: these are the same public client identifiers already documented as "not secret" in `firebase.ts`, and they already ship inside the built JS bundle sent to every visitor regardless.
- **Needs a Web Push certificate (VAPID key) the user has to generate themselves** — Firebase Console > Project Settings > Cloud Messaging tab > Web configuration > "Web Push certificates" > generate a key pair — same category of blocker as the Google Maps API key (tied to their own Firebase project, nothing to guess or placeholder around). Added `VITE_FIREBASE_VAPID_KEY=` to `web/.env` with a comment; `requestAndRegisterFcmToken()` logs a clear console warning and returns `"unsupported"` rather than throwing if it's missing. **Resolved — the user provided a real key**, now set in `web/.env`.
- **Verified as far as this dev environment allows, honestly split between what's proven and what isn't:**
  - Proven end-to-end via real HTTP calls (not just unit-testing the helper in isolation): registered a throwaway victim through the actual `/register` UI, then — since this browser automation environment reports `Notification.permission` as permanently `"denied"` (a sandbox/headless policy, not an app bug — same category of limitation already documented for camera QR scanning and geolocation) — obtained real Firebase ID tokens for temporary admin/volunteer test accounts via the Identity Toolkit REST API (`accounts:signInWithPassword`) and called the actual Express routes directly: `PATCH /:id/verify` (approve), `PATCH /:id/status` (`picked_up` then `delivered`). All three returned 200 with no server-side errors, confirming the notification call sites are wired correctly (right field names, right imports, no crashes) even though no real device ever received a push.
  - Proven directly against `sendNotificationToUser` itself: a user with zero `fcmTokens` no-ops silently; a registered token that fails to send gets pruned from Firestore automatically; both of FCM's distinct dead-token error codes are handled (found the second one, `invalid-argument`, only by testing with a real malformed token and inspecting the actual response rather than assuming one error code covered every failure mode).
  - **Confirmed the real VAPID key actually loads, without needing a grantable permission.** Once the key was added, dynamically imported `notifications.ts` in the live browser console and called `requestAndRegisterFcmToken()` directly: it returned `"denied"` (this sandbox's real, permanent `Notification.permission` value) rather than `"unsupported"` (what it returns when the key itself is missing) — and no "VAPID key not set" warning appeared in the console. This is the correct, provable distinction between "the key isn't configured" and "the key is configured but this sandbox can't grant permission" — the second is an environment limitation, not a bug.
  - **Still not proven, and genuinely can't be from this environment:** the actual browser permission-prompt UI, a real device receiving a real push notification, and the service worker's background-message handler firing — all three need a real browser with notification permission actually grantable, worth a manual phone/browser check now that the key is in place.
  - All test accounts, the test request, and the test donation/delivery created for this verification were deleted afterward via the Admin SDK, same cleanup convention used throughout this file.

## Real SMS (Text.lk)

Reopened after being cut earlier for "needs a paid/verified business account" (Twilio) — the user pointed out **Twilio doesn't actually operate in Sri Lanka** and redirected to **Text.lk**, a Sri Lankan SMS gateway, providing a real API token directly in chat. **Built alongside FCM, not as a replacement** — every trigger point below fires both channels. Built entirely from Text.lk's own published API docs (fetched and read directly, not guessed) since this provider wasn't something already known — confirmed exact endpoint, headers, and field names before writing any code against it, same verify-first discipline as every other external integration in this file.

- **API contract, confirmed from `https://text.lk/docs/send-sms/` directly:** `POST https://app.text.lk/api/v3/sms/send`, headers `Authorization: Bearer <token>` / `Content-Type: application/json` / `Accept: application/json`, body `{recipient, sender_id, type: "plain", message}`. Phone numbers use the **country code with no `+` and no leading zero** (`94771234567`, not `+94771234567` — a different format than Twilio's E.164, worth remembering if this is ever compared against the earlier Twilio version's `toE164()`).
- **`server/src/utils/phone.js`** — `normalizeSriLankanPhone(input)`, a small pure function (no dependencies) that accepts however a user might type a number (`0771234567`, `+94771234567`, `94771234567`, with spaces/dashes) and returns Text.lk's exact expected format, or `null` if it isn't a real Sri Lankan **mobile** number (rejects landlines — the digit after the `94` country code must be `7`). Duplicated client-side in `web/src/lib/phone.ts` and `mobile/src/lib/phone.ts` (same pattern as `districts.ts`) so both registration forms can validate before submitting, not just round-trip to the server to find out.
- **Phone format validation added at registration, server-side authoritative + client-side for immediate feedback.** `POST /api/users/profile` now rejects (400) a non-empty `phone` that doesn't normalize to a valid Sri Lankan mobile number — phone itself stays optional (not newly required), but a value that *is* provided must be real, since a malformed one would previously have just failed silently at SMS-send time with no indication why. The normalized (not raw-typed) value is what's stored in Firestore, so `sms.js` never has to re-guess formatting for freshly-registered accounts. `Register.tsx` (web) and `RegisterScreen.tsx` (mobile) both show the same inline error and a placeholder/hint before submitting. Verified live against the real backend: `0771234567` and `+94771234567` both normalize and save as `94771234567` (201); a landline (`0112345678`) and garbage (`12345`) both correctly reject (400 with a clear message); an empty phone still registers fine (201, `phone: null`) — confirming the field is validated, not newly required.
- **`server/src/utils/sms.js`** — `sendSmsToUser(uid, message)`, mirroring `sendNotificationToUser`'s (FCM) fail-soft philosophy exactly: missing `TEXTLK_API_TOKEN`, a user with no valid phone on file, or a non-`"success"` API response all just log and return, never throw, never block the caller's actual work. No SDK needed — it's one plain `fetch()` call, so there's no equivalent to Twilio's conditional `require()` to worry about.
- **Same three trigger points as before (unchanged from the Twilio version, only the transport changed):** `requests.js`'s `PATCH /:id/verify` and `/bulk-verify` (approve only), `deliveries.js`'s shared `notifyVictimOfDeliveryStatus()` helper (covers both the volunteer-driven `PATCH /:id/status` and the donor's `PATCH /:id/self-deliver`), and `waterLevelAlerts.js`'s `sendAreaAlert()`. Message bodies are deliberately minimal/transactional per the user's own privacy guidance while researching this provider — request ID and status only, never NIC, address, or other sensitive details in the SMS text.
- **`server/.env`** gained `TEXTLK_API_TOKEN` (a real, live token — provided directly by the user in chat, so treated as a genuine secret from the moment it arrived: written straight to `.env`, never echoed back, never placed in any client-side code) and `TEXTLK_SENDER_ID` (defaults to `"AidSystem"`).
- **Deliberately NOT live-tested against the real Text.lk API in this pass — the user's explicit instruction: "build it fully and then lets test at the very end after i say so."** The account has a limited free SMS allowance and the user wants testing to be a deliberate, single decision, not something incidentally burned through during normal build verification. `server/.env` itself carries a comment reminding whoever's in the code next not to trigger a real send (approve a request, mark delivered, etc.) without asking first — the real token being present makes every one of the three trigger points above "live" the moment they're exercised.
- **What was verified before the user's go-ahead:** the server boots cleanly with the real token present (confirms no syntax/import error, and that nothing runs at module-load time); phone validation was fully live-tested end-to-end (see above) since `POST /api/users/profile` never calls `sendSmsToUser` at all — registration and SMS-sending are genuinely separate code paths, so testing one doesn't risk the other's quota.
- **The user then explicitly said to test — one real SMS was sent and delivered.** Sent to a real Sri Lankan mobile number the user provided in chat, via a standalone script mirroring `sendSmsToUser`'s exact request shape (no throwaway Firestore doc needed — the phone number was passed directly). **First attempt failed** with `"Sender ID \"AidSystem\" is not authorized to send this message"` — Text.lk requires custom alphanumeric sender IDs to be requested and approved via their dashboard (Sending tab > Sender ID) first, a few hours to 3 business days per their own docs; this wasn't knowable until actually testing the send. **Second attempt, using Text.lk's own demo sender ID (`TextLKDemo`, taken from their published `curl` example) succeeded**: `status: "success"`, `"Delivered"`, 1 SMS credit consumed. Confirms the entire pipeline is correct — auth token, phone normalization, request shape, delivery — the only remaining gap is `AidSystem` specifically not being an approved sender ID yet.
- **Resolved: `TEXTLK_SENDER_ID` in `server/.env` (and `.env.example`) is set to `TextLKDemo`, not `AidSystem`.** The user's own priority is being able to showcase the feature working (e.g. for the viva), not the exact sender name — since "AidSystem" needs dashboard approval that can take days, `TextLKDemo` (Text.lk's shared demo ID, no approval needed) is the active default so every real trigger point actually delivers right now. Swap to `AidSystem` later if/once it's approved and a branded sender name matters more than immediate demoability.

## River gauges — switched to the official Irrigation Department source

The unofficial community proxy this feature originally used (`slwaterlevel.mevinu.online`) went down mid-session — confirmed directly, not assumed: the domain now 301-redirects to `slwaterlevel.mevinu.com`, and that new domain fails outright (TLS handshake reset over HTTPS, `503 Service Unavailable` over plain HTTP). This is exactly the risk this file already flagged for that service ("could go down or change without notice since it's a hobby project"), just actually happening. Rather than find a replacement hobby proxy, went looking for the real official source directly.

- **Found it by tracing the actual Irrigation Department website, not by guessing.** `irrigation.gov.lk`'s "Reservoirs Status," "Realtime Rainfall Data," and "Realtime Water Level in Major River" pages each turn out to embed or link an **ArcGIS Online Dashboard** (hosted at `slirrigation.maps.arcgis.com`) rather than serving the data as plain HTML. Followed each dashboard's own configuration through ArcGIS's public Sharing REST API (`/sharing/rest/content/items/{id}/data?f=json`, no auth needed) to find its actual underlying data source — not scraping the dashboard's rendered output, reading the same JSON definition the dashboard itself loads.
- **River gauge water levels + flood thresholds live in a real, public ArcGIS **FeatureServer**:** `https://services3.arcgis.com/J7ZFXmR8rSmQ3FGf/arcgis/rest/services/gauges_2_view/FeatureServer/0`. Confirmed this is the Department's own authoritative layer, not a guess — it's the exact same layer their official dashboard's "Alert Level," "Minor Flood," and "Major Flood" widgets read from. Standard Esri REST query semantics (`?where=1=1&outFields=...&f=json`), publicly queryable with no API key.
- **Exact field match for what this feature already needed**, right down to the names: `gauge` (station), `basin`, `water_level`, `alertpull`/`minorpull`/`majorpull` (the three flood thresholds), plus point geometry (lat/lng via `x`/`y`). This is such a close match to the shape the old unofficial proxy returned (`entry.attributes.gauge`, `entry.attributes.water_level`, `entry.attributes.alertpull`, wrapped in the exact `{attributes, geometry}` shape Esri's REST API always uses) that the old proxy was almost certainly just a thin wrapper around this same official service all along — meaning `fetchWaterLevels()`'s existing mapping logic barely had to change.
- **The one real difference: the raw table is a time-series log, not one row per station.** 6,400+ rows and growing (one row appended per reading), so a naive read would return duplicate/stale entries per gauge. Fixed by querying `orderByFields=EditDate DESC` and deduping to the first (most recent) row seen per `gauge` name in JS — same "sort/dedupe in JS rather than a fancier query" convention already established elsewhere in this file (see "Internal case notes"). Confirmed sufficient: the top 1,000 most-recent rows alone contain 40 distinct gauges, matching the ~41 stations this feature has always reported — no pagination needed.
- **`rateOfRise` field dropped** — the old unofficial proxy had a `rate_of_rise` field; this official service doesn't. Checked first whether anything actually used it (grepped the whole repo) — it was only ever declared in `SituationMap.tsx`'s TypeScript interface, never rendered anywhere, so removed it cleanly from that interface rather than leaving a dead, always-null field.
- **UI copy updated everywhere it called this data "unofficial"** — `web/src/locales/{en,si,ta}.json`, `mobile/src/locales/{en,si,ta}.json` (mobile's are verbatim copies of web's, per existing convention), `SituationMap.tsx`'s hardcoded caption, and `mobile/src/components/AreaSeverityMap.tsx`'s hardcoded caption. Sinhala/Tamil updates are AI-translated, not native-reviewed — same caveat as every other translation in this project (see "Internationalization"). The footer's "Data sources" text and the map's own gauge-tab caption both now correctly describe this as live Department of Irrigation data, not a community proxy.
- Verified live end-to-end, not just at the API level: `curl`'d the endpoint directly (40 stations, correct fields, `status: "normal"` for all — accurate, real conditions right now); confirmed no duplicate station names after the dedup logic; loaded `/severity-map` in a real browser, switched to the "River Levels" tab, and confirmed exactly 40 marker paths rendered in the Leaflet overlay pane; clicked one and confirmed its popup read "Panadugama (Nilwala Ganga) — Level: 2.35 m — Normal," matching the raw API response for that exact station field-for-field. Also confirmed the `waterLevelAlerts.js` poller (the other consumer of `fetchWaterLevels()`) runs clean against the new source with no errors.
- **Worth knowing for later:** the same `slirrigation.maps.arcgis.com` account also has a **reservoir water-level/storage dataset** (a published Google Sheet behind the "Major/Medium Reservoirs Status" dashboards, updated daily by the Water Management Branch) — found while researching this fix, not yet integrated into anything. Relevant if the previously-discussed "Dam & Reservoir Risk Monitoring" feature idea ever gets built.

## Water-level area alerts

The second half of the "SMS alerts" ask: notify victims in a specific area when it's disaster-relevant — the concrete example given was "rise if water levels." Came with an explicit constraint the user settled directly rather than leaving to a default: *"lets keep both options open, approved by admin and send automatically in the admin panel with a toggle so admin can choose to automatically send or no"* — so this isn't just the auto-vs-manual choice made once at build time, it's a live admin-facing toggle.

- **`server/src/utils/waterLevelAlerts.js`** is the whole feature's brain. `STATUS_SEVERITY` ranks `normal < alert < minor_flood < major_flood`; an alert only ever fires on a genuine **escalation** (severity strictly increasing since the last check), never on de-escalation and never on staying at the same level — a station stuck at "alert" for hours doesn't spam the same victims every 10 minutes. Verified directly: re-running the check with an unchanged status produced zero new `sentAreaAlerts` entries.
- **`waterLevelAlertState`** (one doc per gauge station, doc id = slugified station name) stores just `{station, lastStatus, updatedAt}` — the minimum needed to detect "did this get worse since last time," updated on every poll regardless of whether an alert fired.
- **District matching reuses the exact same `nearestDistrict()` function** every other district-aware feature in this app already uses (stats-by-area, the requests filter, active-district declarations) — "which victims are in this district" means the same thing everywhere, not a second slightly-different implementation. A district match pulls every victim with an active (`pending`/`verified`/`in_progress`) request whose `location` resolves to that district, deduped by victim.
- **The admin toggle is a single Firestore doc** (`waterLevelAlertSettings/config`, `{autoSend: boolean}`), read fresh on every poll cycle — flipping it in the admin UI takes effect on the very next check, no restart needed. **Defaults to `false` (review-first) when the doc doesn't exist yet** — the safer of the two options is what a fresh install gets without anyone having to remember to configure it.
  - `autoSend: true` → `sendAreaAlert()` runs immediately: notifies every matching victim via the FCM helper from `## Push notifications` above, and logs one `sentAreaAlerts` entry (`source: "auto"`).
  - `autoSend: false` → a `pendingAreaAlerts` doc is created instead (nothing sent yet). `AdminWaterAlerts.tsx` (`/admin/water-alerts`) lists these with **Approve & send** / **Reject** buttons — approving calls the exact same `sendAreaAlert()` function (`source: "admin_approved"`, `approvedBy` recorded), so there's only one code path that actually sends a notification, not two slightly-different ones to keep in sync. Both approve and reject are logged to the audit log (`area_alert.approve` / `area_alert.reject`, extending `AuditLog.tsx`'s existing `ACTION_LABELS`/`describeEntry`).
- **A `setInterval` poller in `server.js`** calls `checkWaterLevelsAndAlert()` every 10 minutes, plus once immediately at startup (same warm-up pattern already used for the HDX district-boundaries cache) — this is what actually detects a rising water level; it doesn't wait for anyone to load a map. It reuses `external.js`'s existing `fetchWaterLevels()` (extracted into a standalone function and attached to the exported router as `router.fetchWaterLevels`, since Express Router instances are just functions and can carry extra properties) rather than making a second HTTP call to the same river-gauge feed the map already polls — same cache, same data, no extra load on the upstream ArcGIS service.
- **New endpoints, all admin-only**: `GET`/`PATCH /api/water-alerts/settings` (the toggle), `GET /api/water-alerts/pending`, `POST /api/water-alerts/pending/:id/approve`, `POST /api/water-alerts/pending/:id/reject`, `GET /api/water-alerts/sent` (last 50, history). No new Firestore rules needed — `AdminWaterAlerts.tsx` uses one-time `apiFetch` calls through these Express routes, not a direct client-SDK `onSnapshot` read, so nothing here is reachable by a signed-in client bypassing the API (consistent with the "lower value to make live" reasoning already used for category/broadcast history views).
- **Verified end-to-end against real infrastructure, but with one honestly-labeled synthetic input.** All 41 real river gauge stations were genuinely at `normal` status at verification time (confirmed by hitting the live feed directly) — there was no real flood to test an escalation against, the same "nothing to demo" situation already hit with GDACS and solved the same way: use real machinery, be upfront that the *trigger* was simulated. Monkey-patched the shared `fetchWaterLevels` export with one clearly-named `"TEST STATION (simulated)"` entry and ran the real `checkWaterLevelsAndAlert()`:
  - With `autoSend: false` (the default): a real `pendingAreaAlerts` doc was created, correctly matched to "Colombo" district via the real `nearestDistrict()` call. Confirmed via the real `GET /api/water-alerts/pending` HTTP route with a genuine admin ID token.
  - Approved it via the real `POST /api/water-alerts/pending/:id/approve` route — `notifiedCount: 3` (correctly found 3 real victims with active Colombo-area requests already in the database, not just the one throwaway test victim created for this check), moved to `sentAreaAlerts` with `source: "admin_approved"`.
  - Flipped `autoSend` to `true` via the real `PATCH /api/water-alerts/settings` route, then escalated the same test station further (`alert` → `major_flood`) — this time it sent immediately with **no** pending item created, confirming the branch actually depends on the live toggle value, not a cached one.
  - Re-ran the identical `major_flood` status a third time and confirmed zero new `sentAreaAlerts` entries — the no-repeat-spam guarantee holds.
  - Also click-verified `AdminWaterAlerts.tsx` itself in the browser (registered a throwaway admin through `/register`): the toggle button flips between "Auto-send: Off"/"Auto-send: On" via real clicks against the real endpoint, and the empty states render correctly.
  - All test accounts, the test request, the test station's `waterLevelAlertState` doc, and the test `sentAreaAlerts` entries were deleted afterward, and `autoSend` was reset to `false` (the safe default) to leave real operation conservative.

## Reservoir risk monitoring

Not from the original feature list — the user pasted an elaborate "Dam & Reservoir Risk Monitoring System" proposal (AI-drafted, citing Irrigation Department sources) and asked whether it was worth building. Answer given: yes, but only once real data access was actually verified — the same discipline already established for DMC/GDACS/river-gauge sources — rather than committing to the large downstream risk-engine/map-layer work the proposal sketched, sight unseen.

- **Found the same way the river-gauge switch was found**: traced `slirrigation.maps.arcgis.com`'s public Sharing REST API (`/sharing/rest/search?q=reservoir&f=json&filter=orgid:J7ZFXmR8rSmQ3FGf`) to two real ArcGIS Dashboards, **"Major Reservoirs Daily Water Level"** and **"Medium Reservoirs Daily Water Level"**, both fed by the same published Google Sheet (`docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/e/.../pub`) maintained daily by the Irrigation Department's Water Management Branch — a different sheet tab per size (`gid=1212294664` for Major, `gid=562386515` for Medium, found by parsing the sheet's own `pubhtml` tab-list JSON). Fetched as CSV (`?output=csv&gid=...`), no auth needed.
- **The dashboards' own point-location layer (`Reservoir_Data_2024` FeatureServer) is NOT publicly queryable** — confirmed by direct testing, not assumed: every layer referenced in the Web Map definition (`Reservoir_Data_2024`, `Canal_System`, `Sluice_2026`, `Scheme_Data_2`) consistently returned Esri's own `{"error":{"code":400,"message":"Invalid URL"}}` for an unauthenticated query, while the same test against the known-public `gauges_2_view` service (the one already used for river gauges) succeeded immediately — ruling out a client-side mistake. **This means there are no reservoir coordinates to plot** — flagged directly rather than faking geocoding or silently dropping the feature. Shipped as a **risk-sorted list**, not a map layer, same "verify what's actually usable, document the substitution" pattern already established for the DMC PDF/GDACS REST API/old gauge proxy findings.
- **`server/src/routes/external.js`'s `fetchReservoirSheet(size, gid)`** parses each sheet's CSV (via the new `papaparse` dependency — quoted fields with embedded commas, e.g. `"5,251"` acres, made naive comma-splitting unusable, confirmed by testing) by **locating columns from the header row's text, not fixed indices** — the Major and Medium sheets don't share identical layouts (Major has an extra "DS IMPACT" column Medium lacks, shifting every column after it), confirmed by parsing both and comparing the auto-detected column maps side by side. Stops at the first non-numbered row, which correctly excludes the sheet's trailing "SUMMARY OF DAILY WATER LEVEL..." recap table rather than misparsing it as more reservoirs.
- **Risk categorization is deliberately descriptive, never predictive** — the user's own proposal said "don't predict gate openings," and `reservoirRiskLevel()` honors that literally: `spilling` (the sheet's own Yes/No flag — already discharging over the spillway, a fact, not a forecast) always wins regardless of %; otherwise `high` at ≥90% effective storage, `elevated` at ≥75%, else `normal`. No trend extrapolation, no "will overflow in N hours" — only what the Department's own bulletin already states as of its `DATE` column.
- **District comes straight from the sheet's own `District` column**, not geo-derived — more precise than the gauge feed's nearest-centroid matching, since these rows already state a real administrative district. One real-data wrinkle handled explicitly: the sheet spells Anuradhapura as "ANURADAPURA" (missing an 'h') — caught by comparing every unique raw district value against `server/src/utils/districts.js`'s real 25-district list and fixed via an explicit `RESERVOIR_DISTRICT_ALIASES` map (not fuzzy matching, so a genuinely new/unexpected name fails visibly as `district: null` instead of silently guessing wrong). A handful of rows have a blank district cell in the source itself — left `null`, correctly excluded from district-based alerting rather than guessed.
- **A real caching bug found and fixed during verification, not just a network fluke**: the first live test showed `GET /api/external/reservoirs` returning `[]` correctly once, then continuing to return `[]` instantly on every subsequent call — because `fetchReservoirs()` originally caught each sheet's fetch error internally and *resolved* to `[]` rather than rejecting, and `getCached()` only evicts a *rejected* promise early (see "Concurrent-load testing" for why that eviction-on-reject exists at all) — a resolved empty array got cached as a legitimate "no reservoirs" answer for the full 1-hour TTL. Fixed with `Promise.allSettled`: only reject (letting the cache evict and retry) when **both** sheets fail; one sheet failing while the other succeeds still caches the partial real data, matching this project's existing "reservoirs partially, not toggle back to empty" resilience convention seen elsewhere (per-city fail-soft in the weather feed).
- **Wired into the exact same alert pipeline "Water-level area alerts" already built**, not a second parallel system — `server/src/utils/waterLevelAlerts.js`'s `sendAreaAlert()` gained a `hazardType: "gauge" | "reservoir"` discriminator (message text and identifying fields branch on it, everything else — victim matching, FCM+SMS fan-out, `sentAreaAlerts` logging — is shared code) and a new `checkReservoirsAndAlert()` mirrors `checkWaterLevelsAndAlert()`'s exact escalation-detection shape (rising risk only, never de-escalation, never a repeat at the same level) against reservoir risk instead of gauge flood status. Shares the same `waterLevelAlertState` collection (`reservoir_` doc-id prefix so a reservoir and a gauge station can never collide) and the **same admin `autoSend` toggle** — one more detector feeding the one pending/sent review queue, not a separate on/off switch to maintain. Polled hourly (`server.js`, since the bulletin itself only updates once a day — no value in checking more often), same startup-plus-interval pattern as the gauge poller.
- **`AdminWaterAlerts.tsx`** (`/admin/water-alerts`) updated to render both hazard types in one unified list — a small `alertSubjectLabel()` helper renders "Rajangana reservoir" vs. "Peradeniya (Mahaweli Ganga)" depending on `hazardType`, and `STATUS_LABEL`/`STATUS_BADGE` gained the three reservoir risk levels alongside the three existing gauge statuses. Pre-existing pending/sent docs (created before `hazardType` existed) render correctly via `pending.hazardType || "gauge"` fallbacks in both the backend approve route and the frontend label helper — confirmed live, since four real gauge-hazard pending alerts from earlier in this project's history were already sitting in the same collection.
- **List panels added in the same two places every other public/official (non-PII) external feed already lives**: `SituationMap.tsx` (admin, `/admin/map`, English-only like its GDACS/earthquake siblings) and `PublicSeverityMap.tsx` (public, `/severity-map`, fully translated — new `severityMap.reservoir*` i18n keys in `web/src/locales/{en,si,ta}.json`; mobile's locale mirrors were **not** updated in this pass, since this feature wasn't built on mobile — see below). Both default to showing only non-`normal` reservoirs (with a "Show all 109 reservoirs" checkbox to reveal the rest) rather than dumping all 109 rows — most are normal on any given day, and the point of a *risk* panel is surfacing what's actually elevated.
- **Deliberately web-only for this pass** — mobile wasn't touched (no `mobile/src/screens` changes, no mobile locale updates), consistent with this project's established practice of building one platform's slice at a time and not silently expanding scope. A reasonable follow-on later, not a gap that was missed.
- Verified end-to-end against the real live bulletin (not synthetic data — no need to simulate an escalation the way gauges/water-alerts had to, since real reservoirs were already genuinely at elevated/high/spilling status at build time): `GET /api/external/reservoirs` returns 109 reservoirs (74 major + 35 medium) with a real risk distribution of 94 normal / 8 elevated / 6 high / 1 spilling (Ambewela, Badulla, 100% and actively spilling); confirmed via direct Firestore inspection that the very first run of `checkReservoirsAndAlert()` correctly created 15 real `pendingAreaAlerts` docs (autoSend defaulting to off) with accurate `hazardType`, `district`, `effectiveStoragePercent`, and message text, and wrote 47 `reservoir_`-prefixed baseline docs to `waterLevelAlertState`; click-verified both `/admin/map`'s and `/severity-map`'s new panels live in the browser (registered a throwaway admin for the admin-side check), confirmed the "Show all" toggle correctly reveals all 109 rows, and confirmed `/admin/water-alerts` renders all 15 new reservoir pending alerts alongside the 4 pre-existing real gauge ones with no crashes or `undefined` text. **Deliberately did not click "Approve & send" on any pending alert** (gauge or reservoir) during this verification — doing so would fire real SMS via the limited Text.lk quota to real matched victims, which this project reserves for a deliberate, explicit go-ahead (see "Real SMS (Text.lk)"). The 15 real reservoir escalations and 4 real gauge escalations are left exactly as found, genuinely awaiting a real admin's review — not test data to clean up. Test admin accounts used for the browser checks were deleted afterward.

### CEB / Mahaweli Complex hydropower reservoirs — a second reservoir source

A direct follow-on, prompted by the user checking the 109-reservoir feed against real-world knowledge and correctly pointing out it's specifically the Irrigation Department's **irrigation** reservoir bulletin — it doesn't (and structurally can't) cover **hydropower** reservoirs like Kotmale, Victoria, and Randenigala, which are a different operator (CEB / the Mahaweli Authority) entirely. The user's own research surfaced a promising lead (`mahawelicomplex.lk` showing a "Major Reservoir Water Levels" section); investigated directly rather than taken on faith, same standing discipline as every other external source in this file.

- **Confirmed real and genuinely public, not a login-walled dashboard**: `mahawelicomplex.lk` 302-redirects to `/login/`, and fetching that page directly (`curl`, no session/auth) returns 200 with a real, current "Major Reservoir Water Levels & Active Storage Percentage — 6:00 AM" section — Kotmale, Victoria, and Randenigala each get a card with a Chart.js bar (Level in MSL) and doughnut (Capacity %), and the actual numbers are baked directly into that JS config at server-render time (PHP backend, confirmed via response headers), not fetched via a separate API call the page makes afterward. This is the best-case scraping scenario: no auth, no rate-limit dance, numeric literals sitting in plain HTML.
- **Coverage gap, found and reported honestly rather than papered over**: only 3 reservoirs get a water-level card on this page. Rantambe, Upper Kotmale, Ukuwela, and Bowathenna appear only in a separate hydro-*generation* table (GWh output), not storage — no level/capacity figure available for them here. Castlereigh, Maussakelle, and Samanalawewa (the Laxapana complex, a different CEB complex) aren't on this site at all; not yet investigated. This is now stated directly in both the code comments and the UI captions rather than implied to be complete.
- **District attribution needed real coordinates, verified rather than guessed** — this source has no district field at all (unlike the Irrigation Dept sheet, which states its own District column). Looked up each dam's actual coordinates via Wikipedia (Kotmale Dam, Victoria Dam (Sri Lanka), Randenigala Dam articles — a genuine external lookup, not recalled from memory) and ran them through the exact same `nearestDistrict()` function every other coordinate-based feed in this app already uses. **One real disagreement surfaced and deliberately left as-is for consistency**: `nearestDistrict()` places Randenigala in Badulla, while several sources describe it administratively as Kandy — the same known nearest-centroid-vs-real-boundary tradeoff already documented for river gauges and the public severity map. Not special-cased for one dam; every district in this app is derived the same way, and consistency across features matters more than one dam's administrative precision.
- **`server/src/routes/external.js`'s `fetchCebMahaweliReservoirs()`** fetches the `/login/` HTML once, then for each of the 3 known reservoirs scopes a regex search to the text between that reservoir's own HTML comment marker (`<!-- KOTHMALE -->` etc.) and the next comment marker — confirmed via direct inspection that these 3 blocks are cleanly comment-delimited with nothing else interleaved, so this can't accidentally pull one reservoir's numbers into another's entry. Extracts `Level in MSL` (the bar chart's `data: [X.XXX]`) and `Capacity %` (the doughnut's `percent: XX.X`) separately. Risk level reuses the exact same `reservoirRiskLevel()` thresholds as the Irrigation Dept feed (≥90% high, ≥75% elevated) — this source has no spilling flag or rainfall figure, so `spilling` is always `false` for these three and risk is judged on capacity % alone.
- **Every reservoir object (both sources) gained a `source: "irrigation_department" | "ceb_mahaweli"` field**, and CEB entries get `size: "hydropower"` and a `levelMsl` field the irrigation ones don't have — directly enabling the UI distinction the user explicitly asked for ("don't call the 109 reservoirs 'all Sri Lankan reservoirs'"). `fetchReservoirs()` was generalized from a 2-source to an N-source `Promise.allSettled` (major, medium, CEB) with the same "only reject if literally every source fails" logic as before, now driven by a labeled `sources` array instead of hardcoded major/medium error-logging.
- **`checkReservoirsAndAlert()` needed zero changes** — it already operates generically over whatever `fetchReservoirs()` returns (name/district/riskLevel/effectiveStoragePercent), so the 3 CEB reservoirs are automatically covered by the same escalation-detection and admin-toggle pipeline the instant they appear in the merged list, confirmed live (see verification below) rather than assumed.
- **Frontend split into two clearly-labeled sub-sections, not one flat list**, in both `SituationMap.tsx` (admin) and `PublicSeverityMap.tsx` (public, translated — new `severityMap.reservoirsIrrigation*`/`reservoirsHydro*` i18n keys replacing the single flat `reservoirsCaption`): **"Irrigation Department Major & Medium Reservoirs"** (the existing 109, with its "Show all" toggle) and **"Hydropower & Mahaweli Reservoirs"** (the 3 CEB ones, always shown in full since there are only 3). A shared `ReservoirListItem` component (`SituationMap.tsx`) / `renderReservoirItem()` closure (`PublicSeverityMap.tsx`) renders both groups' cards identically except for which optional fields are present (`levelMsl` for CEB, `rainfallMm`/`date` for irrigation).
- Verified end-to-end against the real live page: `GET /api/external/reservoirs` returns 112 total (109 irrigation + 3 CEB) with real current figures — Kotmale 695.29m MSL / 69.1% capacity (Nuwara Eliya), Victoria 428.35m MSL / 69.92% (Kandy), Randenigala 215.03m MSL / 19.25% (Badulla), all `riskLevel: "normal"` at verification time; confirmed `checkReservoirsAndAlert()`'s first run against the merged list wrote correct `reservoir_kotmale`/`reservoir_victoria`/`reservoir_randenigala` baseline docs to `waterLevelAlertState` with no errors; click-verified both new "Hydropower & Mahaweli Reservoirs" sub-sections live in the browser on `/severity-map` and `/admin/map`, confirmed correct grouping, correct district/MSL/capacity display, and no crashes. Test admin account deleted afterward.

## SOS / rescue flow

Explicitly requested as its own thing, separate from aid requests: *"'trapped,' 'missing persons,' and 'flood rescue' are life-safety emergencies needing immediate dispatch, fundamentally different urgency from 'I need food.'"* Three real decisions were confirmed with the user before building rather than guessed, since each has real security/UX consequences: **SOS requires login** (reuses the existing auth model rather than opening public unauthenticated writes), **dispatch means status tracking only** (`pending → acknowledged → in_progress → resolved`, not shoehorned into the volunteer/delivery-assignment pipeline — coordinating a rescue isn't "deliver goods"), and **any authenticated role can report one**, not just "victim" (someone could be reporting on behalf of a person they've spotted in danger).

- **`sosRequests`** is a completely separate Firestore collection from `aidRequests` — no shared schema, no priority-score algorithm, no category caps. Fields: `{reporterId, reporterName, reporterPhone, type: "trapped"|"missing_person"|"flood_rescue"|"other", peopleCount, description, location, status, createdAt, updatedAt, acknowledgedAt, resolvedAt}`.
- **`POST /api/sos`** (any authed role) — deliberately minimal required fields (`type` + `location` only; `peopleCount`/`description` optional) so submitting is as close to one tap as this app gets. Every admin is pushed an FCM notification immediately (`sendNotificationToUser` from `## Push notifications` above, fanned out to every `role: "admin"` user) — this is the one place in the app where "someone sees this in seconds" is the actual requirement, not "eventually shows up in a queue."
- **`PATCH /api/sos/:id/status`** (admin) — the four-stage status flow, stamping `acknowledgedAt`/`resolvedAt` once (same "set on first transition, never overwritten" pattern as `aidRequests.verifiedAt`). Notifies the original reporter on every status change too, so they know help is coming without refreshing anything. Logged to the audit log (`sos.status_update`, extending `AuditLog.tsx`).
- **`SosButton.tsx`** — a persistent floating red button rendered in `DashboardLayout.tsx` for every logged-in role, opening a single-screen modal (not a multi-step wizard, which would add friction, not remove it): 4 big type buttons, geolocation auto-captured the instant the modal opens (not gated behind a second "capture location" tap), optional people-count and description fields, one submit button. Fully translated (EN/SI/TA) since this is exactly the kind of high-stakes, high-equity surface the i18n work has prioritized elsewhere.
- **`SosStatusBanner.tsx`** — a live `onSnapshot` listener (own component, rendered in `DashboardLayout.tsx` alongside `EmergencyBanner`) showing the reporter their own unresolved SOS status at the top of every page, colored by urgency. This is arguably the single highest-value live listener in the app — someone who just called for help should see it's being handled without hunting for a page.
- **`AdminSos.tsx`** (`/admin/sos`, top of the admin nav — deliberately above Aid Requests, since this is the most time-sensitive queue in the app) — a live `onSnapshot` dispatch board: a small Leaflet map with colored markers (red pending / amber acknowledged / blue in-progress) plus a list with one-click "Acknowledge" → "Start response" → "Mark resolved" buttons per report, unresolved sorted to the top always. Resolved reports collapse into a small history section below rather than disappearing.
- **New `sosRequests` Firestore rule block added** (`create`: any signed-in user; `read`: the reporter or an admin; `update`: admin only) — this one, unlike the water-level-alerts collections, genuinely needs rules since both `AdminSos.tsx` and `SosStatusBanner.tsx` read it directly via `onSnapshot`, not through the Express API.
- **Verified against the real backend end-to-end via genuine HTTP calls** (temp victim + temp admin accounts, real Firebase ID tokens via the Identity Toolkit REST API, same method already established in this file): a real `POST /api/sos` created a `pending` report; `GET /api/sos` (admin) and `GET /api/sos/mine` (victim) both returned it correctly; a victim attempting `PATCH .../status` correctly got 403; the admin progressed it through `acknowledged → in_progress → resolved`, with all three transitions correctly logged to the audit log with accurate details. All test data deleted afterward.
- **The Firestore rules are now deployed** (`firebase deploy --only firestore:rules`, confirmed with the user first) and verified directly, not just assumed to work because the deploy succeeded: created a real reporter account, a real unrelated victim account, and a real admin account, submitted a real SOS report through the actual `POST /api/sos` route, then made **direct Firestore REST API calls** (bypassing the Express API and the Admin SDK entirely — exactly what `SosStatusBanner.tsx`'s and `AdminSos.tsx`'s `onSnapshot` listeners do under the hood) with each account's real ID token. Results: the reporter reading their own SOS doc → `200` (allowed, correct); the unrelated victim reading someone else's SOS doc → `403` (denied, correct); the admin reading the same doc → `200` (allowed, correct). This is a stronger proof than clicking through the UI would have been — it tests the deployed rule itself, not just the app's convenience wrapper around it. All test accounts and the test SOS doc deleted afterward. **Not yet verified: an actual click-through of `AdminSos.tsx`/`SosStatusBanner.tsx` rendering live data in a browser** — this browser-automation environment hit repeated flakiness on the registration flow specifically (unrelated to this feature; a backend dev-server process kept dying between tool calls), so the wire-level rule verification above was substituted as a rigorous alternative rather than blocking on that. Worth a quick manual look once a session isn't fighting that flakiness, though the rule proof above is the part that actually mattered.

## Community reports (road closures / water conditions)

The second half of the same request as SOS, framed as a specific critique: *"connecting reports to action"* is the crowdsourcing gap the user's own research already identified in Ushahidi — a platform that collected reports but didn't visibly close the loop back into response. Confirmed with the user directly: **submissions restricted to volunteers only** (a smaller, higher-trust pool than opening it to every role, trading coverage for less moderation burden), not victims or donors.

- **`communityReports`** Firestore collection: `{reporterId, reporterName, type: "road_closure"|"water_level"|"other", description, location, district (computed via nearestDistrict() at submission time, same as everywhere else), status: "unverified"|"verified"|"dismissed", createdAt, updatedAt, verifiedAt, verifiedBy}`.
- **`POST /api/community-reports`** (volunteer-only). **`GET /api/community-reports`** (admin, unverified sorted first). **`GET /api/community-reports/verified`** (public, no auth) — the one and only thing the public ever sees; an unverified or dismissed report never reaches anyone outside the admin review queue, so a false or mistaken report can't spread before a human checks it.
- **The actual "connects to action" mechanism, not just a status flip nobody sees: `PATCH /api/community-reports/:id/verify`** takes an optional `activateDistrict: boolean` alongside `approve`. Checking it when verifying writes straight into the **same `activeDistricts` collection** the DMC-alert quick action and `/admin/active-emergencies` already use — a verified volunteer report of "bridge flooded near Kelaniya" can, in the same click, declare that district an active emergency, which immediately feeds the public banner and the victim request form's soft note (see "Active emergency districts" above). This is the concrete, demoable answer to "how does a report actually change anything," reusing infrastructure rather than building a second parallel system.
- **`AdminCommunityReports.tsx`** (`/admin/community-reports`) — unverified reports on top with Verify/Dismiss buttons and the "also declare an active emergency" checkbox, reviewed ones below. One-time `apiFetch`, not `onSnapshot` — same "lower urgency than SOS, doesn't need to be live" reasoning as the water-alerts admin page. **No new Firestore rule needed** — nothing here is read directly by a client via the SDK.
- **Submission UI originally lived inside `VolunteerDeliveries.tsx`** (a form section at the bottom of the volunteer's existing main page) rather than a new nav item — the reasoning at the time was that a volunteer is already there checking their delivery queue, and this is routine reporting, not an emergency needing its own always-visible entry point the way `SosButton` does. **Since moved to its own page — see the follow-on below.**
- **Verified reports surface in two places**, both reusing the existing DMC/GDACS/earthquake list-panel pattern: `PublicSeverityMap.tsx` (translated EN/SI/TA, new `severityMap.communityReports*` i18n keys) and `SituationMap.tsx` (admin, English-only, with a link back to the review page).
- **Verified end-to-end via real HTTP calls** (temp volunteer + temp admin, real Firebase ID tokens): submitted a real report — confirmed it correctly resolved to "Colombo" via `nearestDistrict()` and stayed invisible on `GET /api/community-reports/verified` while unverified; confirmed a volunteer gets 403 on the admin-only list endpoint; approved it with `activateDistrict: true` — confirmed it then appeared on the public verified-reports endpoint **and** that Colombo appeared in `GET /api/active-districts` with `sourceAlertTitle: "Community report: Bridge near Kelaniya flooded, impassable"`, proving the promotion path is real, not just wired-looking. Both `community_report.verify` and the resulting `district.activate` were correctly logged to the audit log. **One honest note on cleanup:** Colombo was already an active district from earlier, unrelated verification work in this project's history before this test touched it — my write refreshed its `activatedAt`/source metadata but didn't change whether it was active (active before, active after), so it was left as-is rather than deactivated, to avoid reverting state this session didn't originally set. All test accounts and the test report itself were deleted afterward.

### Follow-on: report submission moved to its own sidebar page

User feedback, direct: "it doesnt look good in my deliveries" — the report form bolted onto the bottom of `VolunteerDeliveries.tsx` was crowding a page whose actual job is tracking delivery status, exactly the "doesn't look good" complaint. This reverses the original placement decision above, not a bug fix — the original reasoning (volunteer's already there, no need for a new nav entry) turned out wrong in practice once there was real delivery content on the page to compete with.

- **`web/src/pages/VolunteerCommunityReport.tsx`** (new) — the exact same form/state/submit logic, lifted verbatim out of `VolunteerDeliveries.tsx` into its own `DashboardLayout`-wrapped page at `/community-report` (volunteer-only, `ProtectedRoute`). `VolunteerDeliveries.tsx` lost all of the report state/handlers/JSX — it's back to being just the delivery list + availability/location pills, nothing else competing for space.
- **New sidebar nav item for volunteers**: `DashboardLayout.tsx`'s `NAV_BY_ROLE.volunteer` gained `{ labelKey: "nav.reportCondition", path: "/community-report" }` (label "Report Condition") right after "My Deliveries" — a volunteer now has two clearly separated destinations instead of one overloaded page.
- **i18n namespace renamed from `volunteerDeliveries.report*` to a standalone `volunteerCommunityReport.*`** (all three locale files) — `reportSectionTitle`/`reportSectionDesc` became the new page's `title`/`subtitle`, every other key (`reportType`, `captureMyLocation`, `submitReport`, etc.) kept its existing translated value, just moved to the new namespace rather than re-translated from scratch. `volunteerDeliveries.rejectPrompt` (delivery-rejection reason, unrelated to community reports) stayed exactly where it was.
- Verified live (throwaway volunteer account): confirmed "My Deliveries" no longer shows any trace of the report form (`get_page_text` — just the delivery list and availability/location pills); confirmed the new "Report Condition" sidebar link navigates to `/community-report` and renders the form with the correct translated heading/subtitle/options; confirmed the location-capture button is still correctly wired (hits the same documented geolocation-permission limitation as every other page in this browser-automation environment, not a regression). `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npm run lint` clean (0 errors, same 30 pre-existing `any` warnings — the one `catch (err: any)` just moved files, not a new one). Test account deleted afterward.

## Delivery chat + consent-gated contact sharing

Requested directly: a chat between whichever two parties are actually coordinating a specific delivery, plus an explicit opt-in to reveal real name/phone between them. Two design decisions were confirmed with the user before building rather than guessed, since both have real privacy consequences: **mutual consent** (neither side's real name/phone is revealed until BOTH have agreed — not one-sided sharing), and **active-delivery-only availability** (chat opens once the two parties are actually linked, and locks — read-only — once the delivery is confirmed, rather than staying open indefinitely).

- **Which two parties, derived from the existing delivery model, not a new relationship:** a volunteer-delivery delivery gets **two independent chats** — donor↔volunteer (coordinate pickup) and volunteer↔victim (coordinate dropoff) — since the volunteer is the actual intermediary and donor/victim never interact directly in that flow. A self-delivery delivery gets **one** chat — donor↔victim directly — since there's no volunteer in that flow. Never a three-way room.
- **`deliveryChats`** Firestore collection, one doc per (delivery, pair): doc id is the deterministic `${deliveryId}_${pairKey}` (`pairKey` ∈ `donor_volunteer`/`volunteer_victim`/`donor_victim`) — deliberately deterministic so the frontend can construct the id directly from data it already has (a delivery's own id + method), with no extra lookup round trip before opening a chat. Fields: `{deliveryId, requestId, donationId, pairKey, partyAId, partyARole, partyBId, partyBRole, consentA, consentB, contactRevealed, status: "active"|"locked", createdAt, updatedAt}`. **Real name/phone are deliberately never stored on this doc** — the chat only ever identifies the other party by role (e.g. "volunteer") until `contactRevealed` is true, at which point the name/phone are looked up fresh from `users`, not frozen at chat-creation time.
- **`chatMessages`** — top-level collection (not a subcollection), same convention as `caseNotes`: `{chatId, senderId, senderRole, text, createdAt}`, queried by `chatId` and sorted by `createdAt` in JS rather than `orderBy` in the query, avoiding the same composite-index dependency already documented under "Internal case notes."
- **`server/src/utils/deliveryChats.js`** is the creation/locking logic, called from exactly three places: `deliveries.js`'s `PATCH /:id/accept` (creates both volunteer-delivery chats — **at accept, not at assignment/pending_acceptance**, since before a volunteer actually commits there's no real counterpart yet and they could still be swapped for someone else), `donations.js`'s `POST /:id/match` self-delivery branch (creates the donor↔victim chat immediately, since self-delivery has no accept step), and `deliveries.js`'s `POST /:id/confirm` (locks every chat tied to that delivery). All three fail soft — a chat failing to create or lock never blocks the actual accept/match/confirm action it's attached to.
- **`server/src/routes/chats.js`** — `GET /mine` (every chat the caller is a participant in, two queries merged in JS since Firestore can't OR across two different fields), `POST /:chatId/messages` (blocked once locked, notifies the other party via **FCM only, deliberately not SMS** — a chat can generate many messages and this project's Text.lk quota is limited and reserved for the handful of already-wired higher-value trigger points), `PATCH /:chatId/consent` (sets the caller's own flag; no revoke, same one-way-consent convention as everything else in this app; flips `contactRevealed` and notifies both sides once both flags are true), `GET /:chatId/contact` (returns the *other* party's name+phone, but only once `contactRevealed` is true — this is the one place in the app that hands one user another user's PII, and it's deliberately narrow: two fields, gated on mutual consent, resolved fresh from `users` rather than trusting a client-supplied value).
- **New Firestore rule block for `deliveryChats`/`chatMessages`** — both need a live listener (`ChatModal.tsx` subscribes to the chat doc for status/consent state, and to `chatMessages` for the message list), so these rules are load-bearing, not just a safety net. Read allowed only for the two named participants (or admin); **all writes blocked at the rules level for every role, including admin** — Admin SDK bypasses rules entirely, so the Express API still writes fine; this just ensures a client can never bypass the locked/mutual-consent business logic with a direct write. `chatMessages`' rule does a cross-document `get()` on the parent `deliveryChats` doc to check participancy, same pattern already used by `isOwningDonor()` in this same rules file. **Deployed** via `firebase deploy --only firestore:rules`, confirmed with the user first.
- **`ChatModal.tsx`** (new shared component, same modal pattern as `CaseNotesModal.tsx`/`QrScanModal.tsx`) — live `onSnapshot` on both the chat doc and its messages, a "Share my name & phone" button that disappears once *my* consent is given (replaced by "waiting for the other side" until both have agreed, then the real name/phone appear in the header), and a message composer that hides entirely once the chat is locked (read-only history instead). Opened from a "💬" button wherever a delivery is chattable: `VolunteerDeliveries.tsx` (two buttons per delivery — donor and victim), `DonorMyDonations.tsx` (one button, labeled by delivery method), `VictimMyRequests.tsx` (one button per item, translated — new `victimMyRequests.chatWith{Donor,Volunteer}` i18n keys in all three locale files, matching this page's existing translated-page status). All three gate the button on the delivery status actually being chattable (`accepted`/`picked_up`/`delivered`/`confirmed` — not `pending_acceptance` or `rejected`).
- **Verified end-to-end via real HTTP calls** (temp victim/donor/volunteer/admin, real Firebase ID tokens, a full run of both the volunteer-delivery and self-delivery paths): confirmed no chat exists before a volunteer accepts, both chats appear correctly the instant they accept; `GET /mine` correctly lists both for the volunteer; a message sent by the donor lands in Firestore with the right sender/role; a non-participant (the victim, on the donor↔volunteer chat) is correctly rejected with 403; one-sided consent correctly leaves `contactRevealed: false` and `GET /contact` returns `{revealed: false}`; consenting on both sides correctly flips it true and the other party's *real* name and phone come back; confirming the delivery correctly locks both chats, and a further send attempt is correctly rejected with a clear "locked" error; the self-delivery path correctly creates its one donor↔victim chat immediately at match time, with no accept step. All test accounts and data deleted afterward.
- **Also verified live in the browser, both before and after the rules deploy** — this caught something the API-only tests couldn't: before deploying, opening `ChatModal.tsx` for real (throwaway volunteer account, accepted delivery, clicked the "💬 Donor" button) hung forever on "Loading..." with a real `permission-denied` in the console, confirming the rules genuinely are load-bearing and not just a paper safety net. After `firebase deploy --only firestore:rules`, the identical flow — accept, open chat, send a message — worked correctly end-to-end: the modal loaded immediately and the sent message appeared live via the real `onSnapshot` listener, not a page reload. Test accounts and data deleted afterward.
- **A real mistake made while testing this, corrected and disclosed rather than glossed over:** the test script generated random-but-validly-formatted Sri Lankan phone numbers for every test account, including the victim — which meant the existing SMS trigger points (`verify`, `picked_up`, `delivered`) fired real Text.lk sends to a nonexistent number during this test run, burning real SMS credits from this project's limited quota. This directly conflicts with the user's own standing instruction to treat SMS testing as a deliberate, separate decision, not something incidentally triggered by testing an unrelated feature. **Lesson for future sessions: any test account whose flow touches `verify`/`picked_up`/`delivered`/`self-deliver` must have `phone: null` (or an intentionally invalid, unnormalizable value) unless SMS testing is the explicit goal** — a syntactically-valid fake number is not safe just because it's fake.

## Fellow travellers (Phase 1 — visibility + communication)

A volunteer carrying a donation from district A to district B has no way to know another volunteer is making a similar trip — no chance to travel together, or to hand off a delivery if one of them can no longer make it. Requested with an explicit two-phase split: Phase 1 (read-only visibility + reusing the existing consent-gated chat) low-risk and build-now; Phase 2 (an actual handoff of delivery ownership between volunteers) higher-risk, explicitly deferred to its own design review. **Both phases are now built** (Phase 2 in its own follow-on pass, see below, only after its own design was proposed and confirmed — never guessed at). The user's own instruction was to explore first and propose a design before writing any code for Phase 1 — the findings and the proposal below are preserved as they were presented, since the four decisions they surface were genuinely open judgment calls, not something to guess silently.

**Exploration findings (gathered before any code was touched):**
- `deliveries` has no stored origin/destination district field — origin is derived from `donations.location`/`donations.district` (the latter added by the earlier "Donation leftover-quantity tracking" feature), destination is derived from `aidRequests.location` via the existing `nearestDistrict()` util, exactly like every other district-derived feature in this app. Nothing needed to be added to either schema — both constraints ("do not change the donation or request schemas") were honored by construction, not by care taken while writing code.
- `volunteerId` on a delivery is a single field (1:1), and reassignment already exists cleanly (`POST /api/deliveries`'s reassign branch) — but **only while `status === "pending_acceptance"`** (409 otherwise). This is exactly why Phase 2 (transferring an *already-accepted* delivery) is a genuinely different, harder problem than what already exists, not a small extension of it.
- The consent-gated chat mechanism (`deliveryChats`/`chatMessages`, `server/src/utils/deliveryChats.js`, `server/src/routes/chats.js` — see "Delivery chat + consent-gated contact sharing" above) turned out to be **fully generic on role**: `chats.js`'s routes and the Firestore rules for both collections only ever check `partyAId`/`partyBId` against the caller's uid, never what roles those parties are. This meant the entire messaging/consent/contact-reveal mechanism could be reused for a volunteer↔volunteer pairing with **zero changes to `chats.js` and zero Firestore rules changes** — confirmed directly against the deployed rules file before writing anything, not assumed. The one real gap: every existing chat is *system-created* at accept/match time; this is the first chat a user has to trigger themselves.

**Four judgment calls surfaced via `AskUserQuestion` before implementation, and the user's actual answers (all four recommended options — not defaults assumed silently):**
1. **Which delivery statuses count as "active" for matching** → `accepted` + `picked_up` only (excludes `pending_acceptance`, since that assignment could still be rejected).
2. **Origin-district filter** → destination-only by default, origin as an optional toggle (`?sameOrigin=true`) — matches the acceptance criteria's own primary phrasing exactly.
3. **Chat lifecycle** → fellow-traveller chats **never auto-lock**, unlike every other chat type in this app. Reasoning: it's a peer-to-peer volunteer conversation, not tied to one transaction the way donor/volunteer/victim coordination chats are (those lock because the *reason* to talk ends once a delivery is confirmed; two volunteers who've connected have no equivalent expiry).
4. **Platform scope** → both web and mobile in this same pass, matching how every other feature in this project has been built.

**Backend (`server/src/routes/deliveries.js`, `server/src/utils/deliveryChats.js`) — both new, additive, nothing existing touched:**
- **`GET /api/deliveries/:id/fellow-travellers`** (volunteer-only, ownership-checked) — computes the caller's own delivery's origin (`donation.district`) and destination (`nearestDistrict(request.location)`), then scans every other currently-active delivery (`status in ["accepted","picked_up"]`, excluding the caller's own) for a matching destination (origin match only when `?sameOrigin=true`). Runs via the Admin SDK, the same reason `GET /api/stats/district-need` needed one — a volunteer's own client can only read `deliveries` docs where `volunteerId == self` per the Firestore rules, so this matching genuinely can't happen from the client directly. **Response is deliberately anonymized**: `{deliveryId, category, originDistrict, destinationDistrict, status}` — no volunteer name, no donor/victim data. Identity is only ever revealed through the chat below, same privacy posture as every other party pairing in this app.
- **`POST /api/deliveries/:id/fellow-travellers/:otherId/chat`** (volunteer-only) — re-validates the match server-side (both deliveries still active, still same destination) rather than trusting whatever the client's last fetch showed, then calls the new `createOrGetFellowTravellerChat()`.
- **`createOrGetFellowTravellerChat()`** (new, `deliveryChats.js`) — deliberately **not** built on top of the existing `createChat()` helper, because that helper's id scheme (`${deliveryId}_${pairKey}`) assumes exactly one delivery per chat, and this chat spans two independent deliveries with no natural "primary" one. Id is the two delivery ids sorted (order-independent — either volunteer clicking "message" first lands on the same chat doc; verified directly, see below) plus a new `pairKey: "volunteer_volunteer"`. **Stored under new field names, `deliveryIdA`/`deliveryIdB`, deliberately not `deliveryId`/`relatedDeliveryId`** — this keeps the chat structurally invisible to `lockChatsForDelivery()`'s `where("deliveryId", "==", ...)` query, so confirming either underlying delivery can never accidentally lock this chat (a real collision that would have silently violated decision #3 above if the existing field name had been reused). Also unlike every other chat here (system-created at accept/match time), this one is user-initiated — the first such case in this app — but idempotent the same way, via `existing.exists`.
- Everything downstream — `chats.js`'s `/mine`, `/messages`, `/consent`, `/contact` routes, `ChatModal.tsx`/`ChatThreadScreen.tsx` — needed **zero changes**, exactly as the exploration findings predicted.

**Frontend, both platforms:**
- **`VolunteerDeliveries.tsx`** (web) and **`MyDeliveriesScreen.tsx`** (mobile) each gained a "Fellow travellers" section per delivery card, shown only while `status` is `accepted`/`picked_up`. Lazy-loaded on a "Find" button press (not fetched automatically for every card on page load — avoids firing the backend's per-candidate lookup unprompted), with a "match origin too" checkbox toggling `?sameOrigin=true`, and a per-result "💬 Message" button that calls the new POST route and opens the *existing* `ChatModal`/navigates to the *existing* `ChatThreadScreen` with the returned `chatId` — no new chat UI was built at all, only the trigger.
- New i18n keys (`fellowTravellersTitle`/`fellowTravellersSameOrigin`/`fellowTravellersFind`/`fellowTravellersNone`/`fellowTravellersMessage`) added to the existing `volunteerDeliveries` namespace in all three locale files on both platforms (Sinhala/Tamil AI-translated, same not-yet-native-reviewed caveat as the rest of this app).
- `npx tsc --noEmit` clean on both `web` and `mobile` after these changes.

**Verified end-to-end against the real backend** (throwaway accounts — 1 admin, 1 donor, 4 victims, 4 volunteers — real Firebase ID tokens, real HTTP calls against the live Express server, a one-off script written for this verification and deleted afterward, not checked in): built two deliveries heading Kandy→Colombo (different volunteers), one Galle→Colombo, and one Jaffna→Jaffna as a distractor. Confirmed: the Kandy→Colombo pair sees each other by default and the Galle→Colombo one also shows up (destination-only matching); with `?sameOrigin=true` the Galle-origin delivery correctly drops out while the Kandy-origin one stays; the Jaffna delivery never appears for the Colombo group in either mode; a volunteer can't query another volunteer's delivery (403); a delivery still `pending_acceptance` returns `[]` and never appears as a candidate for anyone else; starting a chat from either side of a real match produces the exact same `chatId` (confirmed the sort-based determinism); chatting across mismatched destinations, or with a still-pending delivery, is correctly rejected (400); the resulting chat doc has `pairKey: "volunteer_volunteer"`, the correct two parties, and `status: "active"`; one-sided consent correctly leaves contact unrevealed, both-sided consent correctly reveals the real name; a message sends successfully through the unmodified `chats.js` route. **Regression check included and passed**: the existing donor↔volunteer chat for one of the test deliveries was still created normally at accept, confirming this feature didn't disturb the three existing chat pair types. All 10 test accounts and every document created were confirmed deleted afterward — independently verified via a direct `auth.listUsers()` call and a direct Firestore scan for stray docs, not just the script's own "Cleanup done" log line.

Nothing in the Phase 1 pass assumed or blocked a particular Phase 2 design — the `fellow-travellers` endpoints were purely additive and never touched `POST /api/deliveries`'s existing reassign logic, which is exactly what let Phase 2 build cleanly on top without revisiting any of the above.

## Fellow travellers Phase 2 — handoff

Built as its own follow-on pass, once its own design was explicitly requested and reviewed (`design phase 2 handoff`) — never silently started alongside Phase 1. The three things the user specifically asked to be resolved (what happens to an already-generated QR code, to existing chat history, and to notifications already sent, when the delivery's assignee changes mid-flight) are answered below, each with a concrete mechanism, not left as caveats.

**Three more judgment calls surfaced via `AskUserQuestion` before implementation, all confirmed as the recommended option:**
1. **Handoff target scope** → only a currently-matched fellow traveller (same destination, currently active) — re-uses Phase 1's own validation, not a general "pick any volunteer" tool.
2. **Chat continuity** → lock the old chat pair as a preserved historical record; open a brand-new pair for the new volunteer, rather than swapping identities in place on the same thread.
3. **Handoff request UI** → a live Firestore listener (matching how SOS status already works), not a polled fetch — which meant one new Firestore rules block, confirmed with the user before deploying (see below).

**1. The QR code question — solved by scoping eligibility, not by reconciling tokens after the fact.** A handoff is only ever allowed while the delivery is `accepted` or `picked_up` (`ACTIVE_DELIVERY_STATUSES`, the same set Phase 1 introduced) — a `confirmToken`/QR only exists once status reaches `delivered`, and a delivery can never reach `delivered` and still be handoff-eligible. There is structurally never a QR in play at handoff time, so nothing needs to be invalidated or regenerated.

**2. The chat-history question — the trickiest real wrinkle, since it broke an assumption Phase 1's own exploration relied on.** Chat IDs were deterministic (`${deliveryId}_${pairKey}`) specifically because a delivery's volunteer never changed after acceptance — a handoff breaks that. Fix: `server/src/utils/deliveryChats.js` gained `chatIdFor(deliveryId, pairKey, handoffVersion = 0)`, computing `handoffVersion > 0 ? \`${deliveryId}_v${handoffVersion}_${pairKey}\` : \`${deliveryId}_${pairKey}\`` — **the `handoffVersion = 0` branch is byte-for-byte identical to the original scheme**, so every existing delivery, and every delivery that never goes through a handoff, is completely unaffected. `deliveries` gained one new additive field, `handoffVersion` (default absent/0, incremented by 1 per completed handoff). On a successful handoff: the *old* chat pair is locked via the **existing, unmodified** `lockChatsForDelivery(deliveryId)` (it already only matches chats carrying this delivery's plain `deliveryId` field — which every version keeps regardless of handoffVersion — so it correctly freezes every historical pairing once the delivery is eventually confirmed, with zero changes needed to that function); a **brand-new** chat pair is created at the new versioned ID via the **existing, unmodified call shape** of `createChatsForAcceptedDelivery()`, just now also accepting a `handoffVersion` parameter it passes through to `chatIdFor()`. Considered instead swapping `partyBId` in place on the existing doc — rejected, since old messages authored by the departing volunteer would sit inside what's now nominally the new volunteer's thread, and previously-revealed contact info would need special-case resetting. The existing Phase 1 fellow-traveller chat (volunteer1↔volunteer2, however they originally met) is completely untouched by a handoff — it was already designed to be independent of any one delivery's lifecycle, and a handoff doesn't retroactively invalidate that peer relationship.

**3. The notifications question — nothing to undo, since they're immutable historical events.** New ones fire at the moment each is relevant: to the target volunteer when a handoff is proposed; back to the requester when it's accepted or declined; to the donor and victim once a handoff actually takes effect ("a different volunteer has taken over your delivery"); and to the other volunteer when a pending request is withdrawn.

**Backend (`server/src/routes/deliveries.js`) — a new `deliveryHandoffs` collection and five new routes, nothing existing touched beyond the two additive changes to `deliveryChats.js` above:**
- **`POST /:id/handoff`** (volunteer, must own `:id`) — body `{otherDeliveryId}`, **deliberately not `{toVolunteerId}`**: the first draft took a volunteer uid directly, which would have broken the fellow-travellers list's own anonymity (the client is never told another volunteer's uid, only another delivery's id — see Phase 1 above). Caught and fixed before shipping: the target volunteer is resolved server-side from `otherDeliveryId`, exactly mirroring how the fellow-traveller chat-start route already does it. Re-validates the two deliveries still share a destination district right now (never trusts an earlier client-side fetch), and that no other handoff is already `pending` for this delivery (one at a time, avoiding two volunteers racing to accept the same delivery).
- **`GET /handoffs/mine`** — both directions (sent + received), two queries merged in JS, same pattern as `chats.js`'s own `/mine`.
- **`PATCH /handoffs/:id/accept`** (must be the named recipient) — re-validates the delivery is still owned by the original volunteer and still active (it may have moved on since the request was sent), then: bumps `handoffVersion`, updates `deliveries.volunteerId`, locks the old chat pair, opens the new one, logs `delivery.handoff` to the audit log (extending `AuditLog.tsx`'s `ACTION_LABELS`/`describeEntry` on both platforms), and notifies the original volunteer plus the donor and victim.
- **`PATCH /handoffs/:id/decline`** / **`PATCH /handoffs/:id/cancel`** — both pure no-ops on the delivery itself; only ever change the handoff doc's own status.
- **New Firestore rule block for `deliveryHandoffs`** — read allowed for `fromVolunteerId`/`toVolunteerId`/admin, all writes blocked (API-only), same convention as `sosRequests`/`deliveryChats`. Staged in `firestore-rules/firestore.rules` but **not deployed without checking with the user first**, consistent with every other rules change in this project's history.

**Frontend, both platforms:** `web/src/lib/deliveryChat.ts` / `mobile/src/lib/deliveryChat.ts` (new, small, mirrored) export `deliveryChatId(deliveryId, pairKey, handoffVersion)` — the client-side twin of `chatIdFor()`.
